First, I will identify all possible paths from E to I.
The nodes directly connected to E are B, F, M, and L.
The nodes directly connected to I are H, C, and P.

Let's list the main "branches" of paths from E:

**Branch 1: Paths starting with E -> B**
*   E - B - H - I
*   E - B - C - I
*   E - B - C - O - P - I
*   E - B - C - N - O - P - I
*   E - B - N - O - P - I

**Branch 2: Paths starting with E -> F**
*   E - F - G - H - I
*   E - F - X - G - H - I
*   E - F - X - K - G - H - I
    *   (Note: All paths from F to I must pass through G, and then from G, the only way to reach I is via H. So, H is a critical node for all paths in this branch.)

**Branch 3: Paths starting with E -> M (or E -> L -> M)**
*   E - M - J - P - I
*   E - M - J - D - P - I
*   E - L - M - J - P - I
*   E - L - M - J - D - P - I
    *   (Note: All paths from M (or L then M) to I must eventually pass through P. So, P is a critical node for all paths in this branch.)

To disconnect E and I, I need to sever all these branches.

**Attempt to find a cut set of size 1:**
*   Removing any single node (e.g., B, F, M, H, C, P) will leave other paths intact. For example, removing B leaves paths through F and M. Removing H leaves paths through C and P. Thus, a single node is not sufficient.

**Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
Let's try combinations of two nodes that seem critical:
*   **[B, H]**:
    *   Removing B breaks all paths in Branch 1.
    *   Removing H breaks all paths in Branch 2 (as H is critical for them).
    *   However, paths in Branch 3 (E-M-J-P-I, etc.) are still intact, as they do not use B or H. So, [B, H] is not a cut set.
*   **[B, P]**:
    *   Removing B breaks all paths in Branch 1.
    *   Removing P breaks all paths in Branch 3 (as P is critical for them).
    *   However, paths in Branch 2 (E-F-G-H-I, etc.) are still intact, as they do not use B or P. So, [B, P] is not a cut set.
*   **[H, P]**:
    *   Removing H breaks all paths in Branch 2.
    *   Removing P breaks all paths in Branch 3.
    *   However, paths in Branch 1 that do not use H or P (e.g., E-B-C-I) are still intact. So, [H, P] is not a cut set.
*   Other combinations of two nodes (e.g., [F, M], [F, H], [M, C], etc.) also leave at least one branch connected. For example, removing F and M leaves all paths in Branch 1 (E-B-...) intact.

Since no combination of two nodes successfully disconnects E and I, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

**Finding a cut set of size 3:**
Let's consider the nodes that are critical for each of the main branches identified:
*   For Branch 1 (paths starting E-B), removing **B** will sever all these paths.
*   For Branch 2 (paths starting E-F), removing **H** will sever all these paths (as H is the common point for all paths from F to I).
*   For Branch 3 (paths starting E-M/L), removing **P** will sever all these paths (as P is the common point for all paths from M/L to I).

Let's verify the cut set **[B, H, P]**:
1.  **Removing B**: All paths starting E-B are broken (e.g., E-B-H-I, E-B-C-I, E-B-C-O-P-I, E-B-C-N-O-P-I, E-B-N-O-P-I).
2.  **Removing H**: All paths starting E-F are broken (e.g., E-F-G-H-I, E-F-X-G-H-I, E-F-X-K-G-H-I).
3.  **Removing P**: All paths starting E-M (or E-L-M) are broken (e.g., E-M-J-P-I, E-M-J-D-P-I, E-L-M-J-P-I, E-L-M-J-D-P-I).

Since all possible initial connections from E (via B, F, or M/L) are blocked by removing B, H, and P respectively, no path remains between E and I. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

**Verification using Menger's Theorem (optional but good for confidence):**
To confirm that 3 is the minimum, I can try to find 3 node-disjoint paths between E and I (paths that share no intermediate nodes).
1.  **Path 1 (P1): E - B - C - I** (Intermediate nodes: {B, C})
2.  **Path 2 (P2): E - F - G - H - I** (Intermediate nodes: {F, G, H})
3.  **Path 3 (P3): E - M - J - P - I** (Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P})
The sets of intermediate nodes {B, C}, {F, G, H}, and {M, J, P} are all disjoint. Therefore, there are 3 node-disjoint paths between E and I. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect E and I is 3.

    *   E - M - J - P - I - H. (Uses I)
    *   E - M - L - (covered by E -> L paths)

*   If a path starts E -> F, it must go through F.
*   If a path starts E -> B, it must go through B.
*   If a path starts E -> I, it must go through I.
*   If a path starts E -> L: The only way to reach H from L is via M -> J -> I -> H or M -> J -> P -> I -> H. Both of these paths require passing through node I.
*   If a path starts E -> M: The only way to reach H from M is via J -> I -> H or J -> P -> I -> H. Both of these paths require passing through node I.

    2.  E - B - H
    3.  E - I - H

    Removing B breaks path 2, but paths 1 and 3 remain.

    3.  Remove {B, I}: Path E - F - G - H still exists.

    *   Node I is connected to: H, J, C, P.

2.  **Analyze all possible paths from G to I and group them by their initial segment from G or their final segment to I:**

    *   **Paths through H:** The most direct path is G-H-I. This path can be cut by removing either edge (G, H) or (H, I).

    *   **Paths through B:** All paths from G that go through node B eventually reach I via nodes C or P. Examples include:
        *   G-B-C-I
        *   G-B-O-C-I
        *   G-B-O-P-I
        *   G-B-N-C-I
        *   G-B-N-Y-O-C-I
        *   G-B-N-Y-O-P-I
        To cut all these paths, we can remove the edge (G, B). Alternatively, we could remove the edges (C, I) and (P, I) (2 edges), or a combination of edges leading from B to C/O/N. Removing (G, B) is the most efficient single edge to cut this entire group of paths.

    *   **Paths through E and F:** All paths from G that go through nodes E or F eventually reach I via node J. Examples include:
        *   G-E-L-M-J-I
        *   G-E-M-J-I
        *   G-F-X-K-J-I
        *   G-F-K-J-I
        To cut all these paths, we can remove the edge (J, I). Alternatively, we could remove (G, E) and (G, F) (2 edges), or a combination of edges leading from E/F to J. Removing (J, I) is the most efficient single edge to cut this entire group of paths.

3.  **Formulate a minimal cut set:**
    Based on the analysis above, we can select one critical edge from each group of paths to ensure all connections between G and I are severed.
    *   To cut paths through H: Remove (H, I).
    *   To cut paths through B: Remove (G, B).
    *   To cut paths through E and F: Remove (J, I).

    This gives us a candidate cut set: [(H, I), (G, B), (J, I)]. This set contains 3 distinct edges.

4.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   Removing (H, I) breaks the direct path G-H-I.
    *   Removing (G, B) breaks all paths originating from G through B (e.g., G-B-C-I, G-B-O-P-I, etc.).
    *   Removing (J, I) breaks all paths that lead to I via J (e.g., G-E-M-J-I, G-F-K-J-I, etc.).
    After removing these three edges, there are no remaining paths from G to I.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    To confirm this is the minimum number of edges, we can consider a vertex cut. If we remove nodes H, B, and J, G becomes disconnected from I.
    *   If H is removed, G is connected to E, F, B. I is connected to J, C, P.
    *   If B is removed, G is connected to E, F, H. I is connected to J, C, P.
    *   If J is removed, G is connected to E, F, H, B. I is connected to H, C, P.
    If all three nodes {H, B, J} are removed:
    *   G is connected to E, F.
    *   I is connected to C, P.
    *   Paths from E (E-L-M) cannot reach I because M is connected to J (which is removed).
    *   Paths from F (F-X-K) cannot reach I because K is connected to J (which is removed).
    Therefore, {H, B, J} is a vertex cut of size 3. By Menger's theorem, the minimum edge cut must be at least equal to the minimum vertex cut. Since we found an edge cut of size 3, and the minimum vertex cut is 3, this edge cut is indeed minimal.

    *   All paths starting with H-I must pass through I.
    *   All paths starting with H-E, H-F, or H-G eventually pass through E. Therefore, E is a critical node for this entire branch of paths.

    *   Removing B breaks all H-B-...-O paths.
    *   Removing I breaks all H-I-...-O paths.
    *   Removing E breaks all H-E-...-O, H-F-E-...-O, and H-G-F-E-...-O paths.
    Thus, {B, I, E} is a cut set of size 3.

4.  **Check for Smaller Cut Sets (Minimum Verification)**:
    *   **Size 1 Cut Set**:
        *   If I remove B, paths through I and E remain (e.g., H-I-C-O, H-E-M-J-P-O).
        *   If I remove I, paths through B and E remain (e.g., H-B-C-O, H-E-M-J-P-O).
        *   If I remove E, paths through B and I remain (e.g., H-B-C-O, H-I-C-O).
        *   If I remove any other single node (e.g., C, N, P, J, M, L, F, G, X, Y, D), multiple paths would still exist.

Image name: PG_task3_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are I (black) and L (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect I from L.

First, I identify all nodes directly connected to L. These are M, E, and F. Any path from I to L must pass through one of these three nodes and then use the edge connecting that node to L.

Let's consider the edges directly connecting to L:
1.  (M, L)
2.  (E, L)
3.  (F, L)

If I remove these three edges, L will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from I. This would be a cut set of size 3. Now, I need to determine if a smaller cut (e.g., of size 1 or 2) is possible.

To prove that 3 is the minimum, I will try to find three edge-disjoint paths between I and L. If I can find three such paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

Let's search for edge-disjoint paths:

**Path 1:** I can see a direct path from I to J, then to M, and finally to L.
*   P1: I - J - M - L
*   Edges: (I, J), (J, M), (M, L)

**Path 2:** I can see a path from I to C, then to B, then to E, and finally to L.
*   P2: I - C - B - E - L
*   Edges: (I, C), (C, B), (B, E), (E, L)

**Path 3:** I can see a path from I to H, then to G, then to F, and finally to L.
*   P3: I - H - G - F - L
*   Edges: (I, H), (H, G), (G, F), (F, L)

Now, I need to verify if these three paths are truly edge-disjoint (meaning they do not share any common edges).
*   Edges in P1: {(I, J), (J, M), (M, L)}
*   Edges in P2: {(I, C), (C, B), (B, E), (E, L)}
*   Edges in P3: {(I, H), (H, G), (G, F), (F, L)}

Let's compare the edge sets:
*   (I, J) is unique to P1. (I, C) is unique to P2. (I, H) is unique to P3.
*   (J, M) is unique to P1. (C, B) is unique to P2. (H, G) is unique to P3.
*   (M, L) is unique to P1. (B, E) is unique to P2. (G, F) is unique to P3.
*   (E, L) is unique to P2. (F, L) is unique to P3.

All edges in these three paths are distinct. Therefore, I have successfully identified three edge-disjoint paths between I and L.

According to Menger's theorem, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them. Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

I already identified a cut set of size 3: [(M, L), (E, L), (F, L)]. This set of edges effectively disconnects L from all incoming paths. Since I found a cut set of size 3 and proved that the minimum cut must be at least 3, this cut set is indeed minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, L), (E, L), (F, L)]

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from N.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between H and N.**
    To systematically approach this, I will look for edge-disjoint paths. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

    Let's find three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: `(H, B)`, `(B, N)`

    *   **Path 2:** H - G - E - B - C - N
        Edges: `(H, G)`, `(G, E)`, `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, N)`

    *   **Path 3:** H - I - C - Y - N
        Edges: `(H, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, Y)`, `(Y, N)`

2.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths.**
    Let's list all edges used in these three paths:
    *   From Path 1: `(H, B)`, `(B, N)`
    *   From Path 2: `(H, G)`, `(G, E)`, `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, N)`
    *   From Path 3: `(H, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, Y)`, `(Y, N)`

    Upon inspection, all edges listed above are unique and do not overlap between the three paths. For example, `(H, B)` is only in Path 1, `(H, G)` is only in Path 2, and `(H, I)` is only in Path 3. Similarly, `(B, N)` is only in Path 1, `(C, N)` is only in Path 2, and `(Y, N)` is only in Path 3. The intermediate edges are also distinct.

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem.**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges required to disconnect H from N is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3.**
    To confirm that 3 is indeed the minimum, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects H and N. A straightforward way to do this is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.

    Node N is connected to three other nodes: B, C, and Y.
    The edges connected to N are: `(N, B)`, `(N, C)`, and `(N, Y)`.

    If these three edges are removed, node N becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between H and N. This set of edges has a size of 3.

    Alternatively, I could remove all edges connected to H: `(H, B)`, `(H, G)`, `(H, I)`. This would also isolate H and disconnect it from N, and it also has a size of 3.

5.  **Conclusion.**
    Since we have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths (meaning at least 3 edges must be removed) and found a cut set of 3 edges that achieves disconnection, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)]

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal

Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and H. This is a minimum (s,t)-cut problem, and by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H.

First, I will identify all possible paths between E and H and then try to find a set of edge-disjoint paths.

Let's list potential paths from E to H:
1.  **P1: E - H** (Direct connection)
    Edges: `(E, H)`

2.  **P2: E - F - H**
    Edges: `(E, F), (F, H)`

3.  **P3: E - G - H**
    Edges: `(E, G), (G, H)`

These three paths (P1, P2, P3) are clearly edge-disjoint from each other, as they use distinct edges connected to E and H (or the direct edge).

Now, I need to find more paths that are edge-disjoint from P1, P2, P3, and from each other.
The edges used so far are: `(E, H), (E, F), (F, H), (E, G), (G, H)`.

Let's look at the remaining connections from E: `(E, L), (E, M)`.
Let's look at the remaining connections to H: `(H, B), (H, I)`.

4.  **P4: E - M - J - I - H**
    Edges: `(E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)`
    Let's check if these edges are disjoint from P1, P2, P3:
    - `(E, M)` is new.
    - `(M, J)` is new.
    - `(J, I)` is new.
    - `(I, H)` is new.
    So, P4 is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, P3.

Now, I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths:
P1: `(E, H)`
P2: `(E, F), (F, H)`
P3: `(E, G), (G, H)`
P4: `(E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)`

Let's try to find a 5th edge-disjoint path.
The edges used so far are: `(E, H), (E, F), (F, H), (E, G), (G, H), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)`.

Remaining connections from E: `(E, L)`.
Remaining connections to H: `(H, B)`.

Let's try to construct a path using `(E, L)` and `(H, B)`:
Start from E: `E - L` (edge `(E, L)` is available).
From L: `L - M` (edge `(L, M)` is available).
From M: `M - J` (edge `(M, J)` is **NOT available**, as it was used in P4).

Since any path from E through L must pass through M and then J (as M is only connected to L and J, and L is only connected to E and M), and the edge `(M, J)` is already used by P4, I cannot find a 5th edge-disjoint path.

Therefore, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H is 4.
By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and H is 4.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that disconnects E and H.
A good strategy is to identify "bottleneck" edges that cut multiple paths.
Consider the edges that are the "last" connections to H for the paths identified, or critical edges that cut multiple paths.

Let's consider the following cut set:
1.  `(E, H)`: This cuts the direct path P1.
2.  `(F, H)`: This cuts path P2 (E-F-H).
3.  `(G, H)`: This cuts path P3 (E-G-H).
4.  `(M, J)`: This edge is crucial because it is part of P4 (E-M-J-I-H). It also serves as a bottleneck for any path from E that goes through M or L (e.g., E-L-M-J-...).

Let's verify if removing these 4 edges `{(E, H), (F, H), (G, H), (M, J)}` disconnects E and H:
*   **Direct path E-H**: Broken by removing `(E, H)`.
*   **Paths via F**: E-F-H. Broken by removing `(F, H)`.
*   **Paths via G**: E-G-H. Broken by removing `(G, H)`.
*   **Paths via M**: Any path starting E-M... must pass through `(M, J)` to continue towards H (e.g., E-M-J-I-H, E-M-J-P-O-N-B-H). All these paths are broken by removing `(M, J)`.
*   **Paths via L**: Any path starting E-L... must pass through `(L, M)` and then `(M, J)` to continue towards H (e.g., E-L-M-J-I-H, E-L-M-J-P-O-N-B-H). All these paths are broken by removing `(M, J)`.

Since all identified paths (and any other possible path) are broken by the removal of these 4 edges, this cut set is valid. As we determined that 4 is the minimum number of edges required, this is the minimal cut set.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (F, H), (G, H), (M, J)]

    *   **Path A:** X - G - B - C (Intermediate nodes: {G, B})
    *   **Path B:** X - F - E - L - M - J - P - I - C (Intermediate nodes: {F, E, L, M, J, P, I})

    These two paths (Path A and Path B) are vertex-disjoint. Since I found two vertex-disjoint paths, at least two nodes must be removed to disconnect X and C. This confirms that the minimum cut set size is at least 2.

6.  **Propose a Cut Set of Size 2:**
    Based on the path analysis, I observe that all paths from X either go through node E (from F) or through node G (from F, K, or directly from X).

    According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (min-cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then the min-cut is at least *k*.

    Let's find edge-disjoint paths:

    *   Nodes K and X are only connected to F and G, which are within this cluster.

    *   **Removal of (G, H):** This breaks any path from G to H, and thus any path that starts F-G-H-...-M, or F-K-G-H-...-M, or F-X-G-H-...-M.

        *   All paths starting E -> B are broken because B is removed.
        *   Since all paths from E to I must pass through either F or B, removing both F and B will completely disconnect E from I.
7.  **Verify minimality:** We have shown that a single node removal is insufficient. Therefore, a cut set of size 2 is the minimum.

    *   Edge (L, M)
    *   Edge (L, E)

    Any path from L to J must start by traversing either the edge (L, M) or the edge (L, E). There are no other outgoing edges from L.

3.  **Consider removing a single edge:**
    *   If I remove only (L, M), then paths from L through E (e.g., L-E-B-H-I-J) would still exist.
    *   If I remove only (L, E), then paths from L through M (e.g., L-M-F-K-J) would still exist.
    *   Therefore, removing a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect L from J. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Consider removing two edges:**
    If I remove both (L, M) and (L, E):
    *   Removing (L, M) breaks all paths that start with L-M. Node L is no longer connected to M.
    *   Removing (L, E) breaks all paths that start with L-E. Node L is no longer connected to E.
    *   Since all paths from L must begin with either (L, M) or (L, E), removing both these edges will completely isolate L from the rest of the graph, and thus from J.

6.  **Alternative cut set of size 2:**
    Another valid cut set of size 2 would be [(M, F), (E, B)].
    *   If (M, F) is removed, L-M is still connected, but M is then only connected to L. Thus, M becomes a dead end for any path from L to J.
    *   If (E, B) is removed, L-E is still connected, but E is then only connected to L. Thus, E becomes a dead end for any path from L to J.
    This also disconnects L from J with 2 edges.

Both [(L, M), (L, E)] and [(M, F), (E, B)] are valid minimal cut sets. I will provide the first one found.

Image name: PGS_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are G and I. I cannot remove G or I.
2.  **Identify Neighbors:**
    *   Neighbors of G: {F, H, K, X}
    *   Neighbors of I: {J, P, C, H}
3.  **Explore Paths and Potential Bottlenecks:** I need to find all paths between G and I and then identify a minimum set of nodes whose removal disconnects them.
    Let's try to find vertex-disjoint paths between G and I. If I can find *k* vertex-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove (vertex cut) must be at least *k*.

    Let's try to find three vertex-disjoint paths (excluding G and I themselves):

    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I
        *   Nodes in this path (excluding G, I): {H}

    *   **Path 2:** G - K - J - I
        *   Nodes in this path (excluding G, I): {K, J}
        *   This path is disjoint from Path 1 (H is not K or J).

    *   **Path 3:** G - F - M - L - E - B - C - I
        *   Nodes in this path (excluding G, I): {F, M, L, E, B, C}
        *   This path is disjoint from Path 1 (H is not in {F, M, L, E, B, C}).
        *   This path is disjoint from Path 2 ({K, J} are not in {F, M, L, E, B, C}).

    Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between G and I, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

4.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal actually disconnects G from I.
    Let's consider the nodes {H, J, B}.

    *   **Remove H:** This breaks Path 1 (G-H-I) and any other path that uses H as an intermediate node, such as G-H-B-C-I and G-H-B-N-C-I.
    *   **Remove J:** This breaks Path 2 (G-K-J-I) and any other path that uses J, such as G-F-X-K-J-I, G-K-J-P-I, and G-F-X-K-J-P-I.
    *   **Remove B:** This breaks Path 3 (G-F-M-L-E-B-C-I) and any other path that uses B, such as G-F-M-L-E-B-N-C-I, G-F-M-L-E-B-N-O-P-I, and G-F-M-L-E-B-C-P-I.

5.  **Verify Disconnection with Cut Set [H, J, B]:**
    After removing H, J, and B:
    *   G's remaining neighbors are F, K, X.
    *   I's remaining neighbors are P, C (H and J are removed).

    Let's check if any path exists from G to P or C:
    *   **From G-K:** K is connected to G, X, and J (removed). So, K cannot reach P or C.
    *   **From G-X:** X is connected to G, F, and K (which cannot reach I). So, X cannot reach P or C.
    *   **From G-F:** F is connected to G, M, and X (which cannot reach I).
        *   F-M: M is connected to F, L.
            *   M-L: L is connected to M, E.
                *   L-E: E is connected to L, B (removed). Since B is removed, E cannot reach C or P (as E's only connection to the "right side" of the graph is through B).

    Therefore, with H, J, and B removed, no path exists from G to I.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since we found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. We also found a cut set of size 3 ([H, J, B]) that successfully disconnects G from I. Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

The final answer is:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [H, J, B]

Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between E and H.

1.  **Identify direct connections to H:** Node H is directly connected to nodes G, B, and I. This means any path from E to H must pass through one of these three edges: (G, H), (B, H), or (I, H).

2.  **Identify potential paths from E to H:**
    *   **Path 1 (via B):** E - B - H
    *   **Path 2 (via I):** E - I - H
    *   **Path 3 (via F and G):** E - F - G - H

3.  **Check for edge-disjoint paths:** I will examine the edges used in these three identified paths:
    *   Path 1 uses edges: (E, B), (B, H)
    *   Path 2 uses edges: (E, I), (I, H)
    *   Path 3 uses edges: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)

    Upon inspection, these three paths are completely edge-disjoint. This means they do not share any common edges. For example, (E, B) is only in Path 1, (E, I) is only in Path 2, and (E, F) is only in Path 3. Similarly for the edges closer to H.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

5.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:** To disconnect H from E, I can remove all edges directly connected to H. These are (G, H), (B, H), and (I, H).
    *   Removing (G, H) breaks Path 3 (E-F-G-H) and any other path ending at G-H.
    *   Removing (B, H) breaks Path 1 (E-B-H) and any other path ending at B-H.
    *   Removing (I, H) breaks Path 2 (E-I-H) and any other path ending at I-H.

    After removing these three edges, node H becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, ensuring no path can exist between E and H.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since 3 edges are necessary (due to 3 edge-disjoint paths) and 3 edges are sufficient (by removing the edges directly connected to H), the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, H), (B, H), (I, H)]

Let's list some paths from G to I:

1.  **Path 1:** G - H - I
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding G and I): {H}

2.  **Path 2:** G - B - C - I
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding G and I): {B, C}

3.  **Path 3:** G - E - L - M - J - P - O - I
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding G and I): {E, L, M, J, P, O}

Now, let's check if these three paths are node-disjoint (meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes).
*   Path 1 uses node H.
*   Path 2 uses nodes B and C.
*   Path 3 uses nodes E, L, M, J, P, and O.

Indeed, the sets of intermediate nodes {H}, {B, C}, and {E, L, M, J, P, O} are all mutually exclusive. Therefore, these three paths are node-disjoint.

Since there are at least three node-disjoint paths between G and I, according to Menger's Theorem, a minimum of 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect them.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3 that actually disconnects G and I.
Let's try to remove one node from each of these three node-disjoint paths.

Consider the cut set: **[H, B, P]**

1.  **Removing H:** This breaks Path 1 (G - H - I).
2.  **Removing B:** This breaks Path 2 (G - B - C - I) and all other paths that go through B, such as:
    *   G - B - N - C - I
    *   G - B - N - Y - O - C - I
    *   G - B - N - Y - O - I
    *   Also, any paths from G that first go through E or F and then B (e.g., G - E - B - C - I, G - F - E - B - C - I) are broken.
3.  **Removing P:** This breaks Path 3 (G - E - L - M - J - P - O - I) and any other paths that go through P, such as G - F - E - L - M - J - P - O - I.

Let's verify that no path remains after removing H, B, and P:
*   **G's remaining direct neighbors:** F, E. (H and B are removed)
*   **I's remaining direct neighbors:** C, O. (H and P are removed)

    *   E -> L: (covered above)

    *   Visited: `{L}`

    *   Dequeue `(L, [L])`.
    *   Neighbors of L: E, M.
    *   Add `(E, [L, E])` to queue. Mark E as visited.
    *   Add `(M, [L, M])` to queue. Mark M as visited.
    *   Queue: `[(E, [L, E]), (M, [L, M])]`
    *   Visited: `{L, E, M}`

    *   Dequeue `(E, [L, E])`.
        *   Neighbors of E: L (visited), M (visited), F, G, B.
        *   Add `(F, [L, E, F])` to queue. Mark F as visited.

**Nodes directly connected to C:** B, O, I, N.

1.  X-F-E-B-C

**Paths starting with X-G:**
9.  X-G-B-C
10. X-G-H-I-C
11. X-G-H-I-P-O-C

Now, I need to find a minimal set of edges whose removal breaks all these paths. I will look for "bottleneck" edges that are part of multiple paths.

Let's consider the main "branches" or "gateways" from the X-side of the graph to the C-side.
There appear to be three primary ways for paths to flow from the vicinity of X towards C:
1.  Through the edge (F, E), which leads to B or further out to J and P.
2.  Through the edge (G, B), which leads directly to B.
3.  Through the edge (H, I), which leads to I and then to C or P-O-C.

Let's propose a cut set based on these observations: **[(F, E), (G, B), (H, I)]**. This set contains 3 edges.

Now, I will verify if removing these three edges disconnects all paths listed above:

*   **Path Group 1 (X-F-E...):**
    *   X-F-E-B-C: Broken by removing (F, E).
    *   X-F-E-L-M-J-P-O-C: Broken by removing (F, E).

    *   X-F-G-H-I-P-O-C: Broken by removing (H, I).

    *   X-G-H-I-P-O-C: Broken by removing (H, I).

All identified paths from X to C are broken by the removal of edges (F, E), (G, B), and (H, I).

Next, I must prove that this is the *minimum* number of edges. This means showing that removing any two of these three edges would leave at least one path intact.

1.  **If (F, E) and (G, B) are removed:**

Since removing any two of the edges in the proposed cut set leaves at least one path between X and C, it is impossible to disconnect them with only two edges. Therefore, three edges are the minimum required.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node B (black) is connected to H, C, N.
    *   Node M (black) is connected to E, L, J.

2.  **Consider a simple cut strategy: Isolate one of the target nodes.**
    *   To isolate M, I can remove all edges directly connected to it. These are (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M).
    *   If these three edges are removed, M becomes completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, and thus from B.
    *   This forms a cut set of size 3: `[(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]`.
    *   Similarly, to isolate B, I can remove all edges directly connected to it: (B, H), (B, C), (B, N). This also forms a cut set of size 3: `[(B, H), (B, C), (B, N)]`.
    *   Since we found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3. Now, I need to determine if a smaller cut (size 1 or 2) is possible.

3.  **Check for a cut of size 1:**
    *   If I remove any single edge, will B and M be disconnected?
    *   For example, removing (E, M) leaves paths like B-H-E-L-M or B-C-I-J-M.
    *   Removing (B, H) leaves paths like B-C-I-J-M or B-N-O-P-J-M.
    *   It's clear that removing a single edge will not disconnect B and M, as there are multiple alternative paths.

4.  **Check for a cut of size 2:**
    *   To prove that 2 edges are not sufficient, I need to show that for any pair of edges removed, there is still at least one path between B and M.
    *   **Case 1: Remove two edges directly connected to M.**
        *   If I remove (E, M) and (L, M): M is still connected to J. Paths like B-H-I-J-M or B-C-I-J-M still exist.
        *   If I remove (E, M) and (J, M): M is still connected to L. The path B-H-E-L-M still exists.
        *   If I remove (L, M) and (J, M): M is still connected to E. Paths like B-H-E-M or B-H-G-F-E-M still exist.
        *   Therefore, removing any two edges directly connected to M is not sufficient.
    *   **Case 2: Remove two edges directly connected to B.**
        *   If I remove (B, H) and (B, C): B is still connected to N. Paths like B-N-O-P-J-M still exist.
        *   If I remove (B, H) and (B, N): B is still connected to C. Paths like B-C-I-J-M still exist.
        *   If I remove (B, C) and (B, N): B is still connected to H. Paths like B-H-E-M still exist.
        *   Therefore, removing any two edges directly connected to B is not sufficient.
    *   **Case 3: Remove two "bridge" edges (edges that connect the "B-side" of the graph to the "M-side").**
        *   Let's identify some key "bridge" edges: (H, E), (H, I), (C, I), (O, P), (F, E).
        *   Consider removing (H, E) and (C, I):
            *   Paths through H and I (e.g., B-H-I-J-M) are still connected via (H, I).
            *   Paths through C and O (e.g., B-C-O-P-J-M) are still connected via (C, O) and (O, P).
            *   Paths through N (e.g., B-N-O-P-J-M) are still connected.
            *   So, this pair is not sufficient.
        *   Consider removing (H, I) and (J, M):
            *   Paths through E (e.g., B-H-E-M, B-H-G-F-E-M) are still connected.
            *   So, this pair is not sufficient.

5.  **Conclusion on minimum cut size:**
    *   Since a cut of size 1 or 2 is not sufficient, and a cut of size 3 (e.g., `[(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]`) is sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

        *   M - J - P - O - N

    *   **Path 1:** M - E - H - B - N
        Intermediate nodes: {E, H, B}

    *   **Path 2:** M - J - P - O - N
        Intermediate nodes: {J, P, O}

    Now, let's check if there's a third vertex-disjoint path that doesn't use any of {E, H, B, J, P, O}.
    From M, the only remaining neighbor not used in P1 or P2 is L.
    *   M - L - E... (E is already used in Path 1, so this branch is blocked).

    Since we could only find two vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

5.  **Identify a cut set of size 2:**

    *   From Path 1 (M - E - H - B - N), we can choose E, H, or B.
    *   From Path 2 (M - J - P - O - N), we can choose J, P, or O.

    Let's try the combination [E, J]:
    *   Removing E: This breaks all paths that go through E. From the path list above, this includes all paths starting M-L-E... and M-E... (paths 1-8).
    *   Removing J: This breaks all paths that go through J. From the path list above, this includes all paths starting M-J... (paths 9-12).

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, F and J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are F and J.

2.  **Analyze connections from F:**
    *   Node F is directly connected to E, X, and K.
    *   All paths from F to J must start by traversing one of these three edges: (F, E), (F, X), or (F, K).

3.  **Analyze connections to J:**
    *   Node J is directly connected to M, P, and D.
    *   All paths from F to J must end by traversing one of these three edges: (M, J), (P, J), or (D, J).

4.  **Consider potential cut sets:**
    *   A straightforward approach is to remove all edges connected to one of the target nodes. For example, removing (M, J), (P, J), and (D, J) would isolate J, requiring 3 edges. Similarly, removing (F, E), (F, X), and (F, K) would isolate F, also requiring 3 edges. This suggests the minimum cut is at most 3.

5.  **Look for a smaller cut (size 2):**
    To find a cut of size 2, we need to identify two edges that are part of *all* possible paths between F and J. This means these two edges must act as "bottlenecks" for all routes.

    Let's examine the structure of the graph around F and how paths branch out:
    *   **Branch 1 (via E):** F is connected to E. From E, the only ways to proceed towards J are via M (E-M) or via L then M (E-L-M). Both of these paths eventually lead to M. Therefore, the edge (E, M) is critical for all paths from F that go through E. If (E, M) is removed, F cannot reach M via E, and thus cannot reach J via E.

    *   **Branch 2 (via X or K):** F is connected to X and K.
        *   From X, the only connection (other than F) is to K.
        *   From K, the only connections (other than F and X) are to G.
        *   From G, the only connections (other than X and K) are to H.
        *   This means any path from F that goes through X or K *must* pass through G and then H to proceed further into the graph towards J.
        *   Therefore, the edge (K, G) is critical for all paths from F that go through K or X. If (K, G) is removed, F cannot reach G from K (or X-K), and thus cannot reach J via this branch.

6.  **Propose a cut set of size 2:**
    Based on the analysis above, consider removing the edges **(E, M)** and **(K, G)**.

7.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   **Removing (E, M):**
        *   This directly breaks the path F-E-M-J.
        *   It also breaks the path F-E-L-M-J, because L is only connected to E and M. If (E, M) is removed, M is disconnected from E (and L, which is connected to E). Thus, all paths from F through E to J are severed.

    *   **Removing (K, G):**
        *   This directly breaks the path F-K-G-...-J.
        *   It also breaks the path F-X-K-G-...-J.
        *   Nodes K and X are only connected to G (and F, and each other) to proceed towards J. If (K, G) is removed, K (and by extension X) cannot reach G. Thus, all paths from F through K or X to J are severed.

    Since all paths from F to J must pass through either the E-M connection or the K-G connection, removing these two edges completely disconnects F from J.

8.  **Confirm minimality:**
    We have found a cut set of size 2. Since removing only one edge would leave at least one path (e.g., removing (E, M) leaves paths through K/X/G/H, and removing (K, G) leaves paths through E/M), a single edge is not sufficient. Therefore, 2 is the minimum number of edges.

The final answer is based on the reasoning above.

The two edges that must be removed are (E, M) and (K, G).

        *   Intermediate nodes: {L, F, X, K, G, H}

    *   Set 1: {M, J, P}
    *   Set 2: {B, C}

    These three sets of intermediate nodes are completely disjoint. This means that these three paths do not share any common nodes (other than the start node E and end node I).

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of vertices that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices (the vertex-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and J.

1.  **Identify direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node E is connected to L, M, F, B.
    *   Node J is connected to D, K, I, P.

2.  **Consider all possible paths from E to J:**
    To disconnect E from J, we need to ensure that no path exists between them. I will analyze the "entry points" to J from the rest of the graph. These are the nodes directly connected to J: D, K, I, P.

    *   **Paths through K to J (via edge (K, J)):**
        *   E - F - K - J
        *   E - F - G - K - J
        *   E - F - X - K - J
        *   E - M - F - K - J
        *   E - M - F - G - K - J
        *   E - M - F - X - K - J
        All these paths rely on the edge (K, J) to reach J.

    *   **Paths through I to J (via edge (I, J)):**
        *   E - F - G - H - I - J
        *   E - M - F - G - H - I - J
        *   E - B - H - I - J
        *   E - B - N - C - I - J
        *   E - B - C - I - J
        All these paths rely on the edge (I, J) to reach J.

    *   **Paths through P to J (via edge (P, J)):**
        *   E - B - N - O - P - J
        *   E - B - C - O - P - J
        All these paths rely on the edge (P, J) to reach J.

    *   **Paths through D to J (via edge (D, J)):**
        Node D is connected to J and P. Any path from E to D must first pass through P (e.g., E - B - N - O - P - D - J, but D is not connected to P in the image, only J and P are connected to each other, so D-P is an edge). Let's re-check D's connections. D is connected to J and P. So, a path to D would be E -> ... -> P -> D -> J. This path would be covered by cutting (P, J) or (D, J). However, there is no direct path from E to D that doesn't involve J or P. If we cut (P, J), then D is only connected to J. If we cut (D, J), then D is isolated from J. Since P is already an "entry point" to J, we can consider paths through D as being covered by the P-channel or by cutting (D, J).

3.  **Identify a potential minimal cut set:**
    To disconnect J from all paths originating from E, we must cut at least one edge from each of the distinct "channels" leading to J. The three primary channels are through nodes K, I, and P.
    The edges (K, J), (I, J), and (P, J) are the final links for these three distinct sets of paths to reach J.

    Let's propose the cut set: `[(K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]`. This set contains 3 edges.

4.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   Removing (K, J) breaks all paths that go through K to reach J.
    *   Removing (I, J) breaks all paths that go through I to reach J.
    *   Removing (P, J) breaks all paths that go through P to reach J.
    After removing these three edges, J is only connected to D. Node D is connected to J and P. Since (P, J) is removed, D is effectively only connected to J. There are no paths from E to D that do not involve J or P. Therefore, J is completely disconnected from E.

5.  **Prove minimality:**
    We need to show that it's impossible to disconnect E and J by removing only 2 edges.
    Consider the three distinct paths identified:
    *   Path 1: E - F - K - J
    *   Path 2: E - B - H - I - J
    *   Path 3: E - B - N - O - P - J

    These three paths are "edge-disjoint" with respect to the edges (K, J), (I, J), and (P, J).
    *   If we remove any two edges from the set `{(K, J), (I, J), (P, J)}`, the third path will remain intact. For example, removing (K, J) and (I, J) leaves Path 3 (E - B - N - O - P - J) open.
    *   If we remove two edges that are not from this set, it's even less likely to disconnect all paths. For instance, removing (E, F) and (E, B) would still leave paths like E - M - F - K - J or E - M - F - G - H - I - J.

    Since at least three edges are required to cut these three distinct path bundles, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M and B. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect M from B.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are M and B.

2.  **Identify direct connections to M and B:**
    *   Node M is directly connected to J, O, and L.
    *   Node B is directly connected to N, C, E, and O.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum number of edges to remove (min-cut), we can look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between M and B (max-flow min-cut theorem).
    Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths from M to B:

    *   **Path 1:** M - O - B
        Edges: (M, O), (O, B)

    *   **Path 2:** M - L - E - B
        Edges: (M, L), (L, E), (E, B)

    *   **Path 3:** M - J - I - C - B
        Edges: (M, J), (J, I), (I, C), (C, B)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    Let's list all edges used in these three paths:
    *   Path 1: (M, O), (O, B)
    *   Path 2: (M, L), (L, E), (E, B)
    *   Path 3: (M, J), (J, I), (I, C), (C, B)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed are unique and do not overlap between the three paths. For example, (M, O) is distinct from (M, L) and (M, J). Similarly, (O, B) is distinct from (L, E), (E, B), (J, I), (I, C), and (C, B), and so on for all edges.
    Therefore, we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B.

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's Theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

6.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Consider removing all edges directly connected to node M:
    *   (M, J)
    *   (M, O)
    *   (M, L)

    If these three edges are removed, node M becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from M to B. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since we have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths (implying a minimum cut of at least 3) and found a cut set of exactly 3 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, M), (L, M), (M, O)]

        *   M - L - E - H - I - P - O - N (This path eventually goes through E)

3.  **Analyze potential cut sets:**

    *   **Can we disconnect M and N by removing only 1 edge?**
        No. If we remove any single edge, there will always be at least one alternative path remaining. For example, removing (M, J) still leaves paths through E (e.g., M-E-H-B-N). Removing an edge further down the graph (e.g., (H, B)) would leave many other paths intact. Therefore, a cut of size 1 is not possible.

    *   **Consider removing edges directly connected to M:**
        Node M has three direct connections: (M, E), (M, L), and (M, J).
        If we remove (M, E) and (M, J):
        *   M is now only connected to L.
        *   L is connected to M and E. Since (M, E) has been removed, L is no longer connected to E.
        *   Therefore, L is only connected to M, and M is only connected to L.
        *   This effectively isolates the component {M, L} from the rest of the graph.
        *   No path can exist from M to N.
        This is a valid cut set of size 2: [(M, E), (M, J)].

    *   **Consider removing edges directly connected to N:**
        Node N has four direct connections: (N, B), (N, C), (N, O), and (N, Y). Removing all of them would isolate N, but this would require 4 edges, which is more than 2.

    *   **Consider other cut sets of size 2 (not directly connected to M or N):**
        All paths from M must pass through either J or E.
        If we don't cut (M, J) or (M, E), we would need to cut all paths originating from J *and* all paths originating from E.
        *   To cut all paths from J to N, we would need at least two edges (e.g., (J, P) and (J, I)).
        *   To cut all paths from E to N, we would need at least one edge (e.g., (E, H)).
        This would require at least 2 + 1 = 3 edges, which is more than the 2 edges identified above.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges to remove is 2. The cut set [(M, E), (M, J)] successfully disconnects M from N by isolating M (and L) from the rest of the graph.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(M, E), (M, J)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['M', 'E'], ['M', 'J'], ['M', 'L']]]}

Image name: POGBb_task2_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are O and H.
2.  **Understand the goal:** Find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect O and H, without removing O or H themselves.
3.  **Initial assessment (Cut Set of size 1 or 2?):**
    *   I first consider if a single node removal can disconnect O and H. By inspecting the graph, it's clear that O and H are highly connected, with multiple distinct routes. Removing any single node (e.g., P, C, Y, I, B, G, N, E, M, J, L, F, K, D, X) would leave many alternative paths open. Thus, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.
    *   Next, I consider if a cut set of size 2 is possible. I tried several combinations of two nodes (e.g., {I, B}, {P, C}, {N, E}, {P, B}, {C, G}, {Y, I}, {M, N}, {J, N}). In each case, I found at least one path remaining between O and H. For example, removing {I, B} still leaves paths like O-P-J-M-L-E-F-K-G-H. This suggests that a cut set of size 2 might not be sufficient.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Vertex-Disjoint Paths):** Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. If I can find 3 vertex-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut set size is 3.

    Let's find vertex-disjoint paths from O to H:
    *   **Path 1:** O - P - J - M - L - E - F - K - G - H
        Intermediate nodes used: {P, J, M, L, E, F, K, G}
    *   **Path 2:** O - C - I - H
        Intermediate nodes used: {C, I}
        (This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1, as {C, I} are not in {P, J, M, L, E, F, K, G})
    *   **Path 3:** O - Y - N - B - H
        Intermediate nodes used: {Y, N, B}
        (This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2, as {Y, N, B} are not in {P, J, M, L, E, F, K, G} or {C, I})

    Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between O and H, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect them is 3.

5.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now that I know the minimum is 3, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects O and H.
    Let's consider the direct neighbors of O: {P, C, Y}. If we remove all three, O is isolated. This is a cut set of size 3.
    Let's consider the direct neighbors of H: {I, B, G}. If we remove all three, H is isolated. This is a cut set of size 3.

    Let's try to find a cut set that is not just the direct neighbors. Consider the nodes {P, C, N}.
    *   If **P** is removed, all paths starting O-P-... are broken. This includes Path 1 (O-P-J-M-L-E-F-K-G-H) and O-P-I-H, etc.
    *   If **C** is removed, all paths starting O-C-... are broken. This includes Path 2 (O-C-I-H) and O-C-B-H, O-C-N-B-H, etc. Also, paths like O-Y-N-C-I-H and O-Y-N-C-B-H are broken.
    *   If **N** is removed, all paths starting O-Y-N-... are broken. This includes Path 3 (O-Y-N-B-H). Also, paths like O-C-N-B-H, O-Y-N-C-I-H, O-Y-N-C-B-H are broken (some already broken by C).

    After removing P, C, and N:
    *   Any path starting O-P is blocked by P.
    *   Any path starting O-C is blocked by C.
    *   Any path starting O-Y must go through N (O-Y-N...). Since N is removed, these paths are blocked.
    Therefore, all possible paths from O to H are broken.

6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3, and {P, C, N} is a valid cut set.

The final answer is $\boxed{\text{Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [P, C, N]}}$

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and H.
2.  **Identify direct connections to H:** Any path reaching H must come from one of its direct neighbors: G, B, or I. Therefore, to completely disconnect H from E (and the rest of the graph), all paths leading into H must be severed. This implies that any cut set must include nodes that block all routes through G, B, and I.
3.  **Consider a cut set of size 3:** If we remove nodes G, B, and I, then H will have no remaining connections to any other white nodes. This would effectively disconnect H from E. Thus, {G, B, I} is a valid cut set of size 3.
4.  **Determine if a smaller cut set is possible (1 node):**
    *   If we remove G: Paths like E-B-H and E-L-M-J-P-I-H would still exist.
    *   If we remove B: Paths like E-G-H and E-L-M-J-P-I-H would still exist.
    *   If we remove I: Paths like E-G-H and E-B-H would still exist.
    *   Removing any other single node would also leave multiple paths intact (e.g., removing L would still leave E-G-H and E-B-H).
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.
5.  **Determine if a smaller cut set is possible (2 nodes):**
    *   Consider removing any two nodes from the set {G, B, I}:
        *   Removing {G, B}: The path E-L-M-J-P-I-H still exists.
        *   Removing {G, I}: The path E-B-H still exists.
        *   Removing {B, I}: The path E-G-H still exists.
    *   Since removing any two of these critical nodes still leaves a path to H, a 2-node cut consisting of only these nodes is not sufficient.
    *   Consider removing any other pair of nodes. For example, if we remove L and M, paths like E-G-H and E-B-H still exist.
    *   Therefore, a two-node removal is not sufficient.
6.  **Conclusion:** Since a 1-node cut is not possible and a 2-node cut is not possible, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3. As we identified {G, B, I} as a valid cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

To further confirm, we can identify three node-disjoint paths between E and H (excluding E and H themselves):
1.  E - G - H (intermediate node: G)
2.  E - B - H (intermediate node: B)
3.  E - L - M - J - P - I - H (intermediate nodes: L, M, J, P, I)
Since these three paths are node-disjoint, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect E and H.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [G, B, I]

    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that it's impossible to disconnect G and N by removing only 1 or 2 edges. This can be done by finding a set of edge-disjoint paths between G and N. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then at least *k* edges must be removed to disconnect the nodes (by Menger's Theorem).

    Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from G to N:

    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I - C - N
        Edges: (G, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)

    *   **Path 2:** G - E - B - N
        Edges: (G, E), (E, B), (B, N)

    *   **Path 3:** G - F - E - L - M - J - P - O - Y - N
        Edges: (G, F), (F, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y), (Y, N)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   **Path 1 edges:** (G, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   **Path 2 edges:** (G, E), (E, B), (B, N)
    *   **Path 3 edges:** (G, F), (F, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y), (Y, N)

    Let's check for common edges:
    *   The starting edges from G are (G, H), (G, E), (G, F) – these are all distinct.
    *   The edges (H, I), (I, C), (C, N) are distinct from any edges in Path 2 or Path 3.
    *   The edges (E, B), (B, N) are distinct from any edges in Path 1 or Path 3.
    *   The edges (F, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y), (Y, N) are distinct from any edges in Path 1 or Path 2. (Note: (F,E) and (E,B) are distinct, (E,L) and (E,B) are distinct. (Y,N) and (B,N) and (C,N) are distinct edges connected to N).

    All three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and N, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.
    I have also identified a cut set of 3 edges: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)]. Removing these three edges isolates N, thereby disconnecting it from G.

    Since we found a cut set of size 3 and proved that at least 3 edges are required, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

The target nodes are H (black) and N (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from N.

1.  **Identify the direct connections of the target nodes:**
    *   Node H is connected to G, I, and B.
    *   Node N is connected to B, C, and O.

2.  **Consider simple cut sets:**
    *   If I remove all edges connected to H: (H, G), (H, I), (H, B). This would be 3 edges. After removing these, H is isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from N.
    *   If I remove all edges connected to N: (N, B), (N, C), (N, O). This would also be 3 edges. After removing these, N is isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from H.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges) is possible:**
    *   To disconnect H from N, I need to cut all paths between them.
    *   According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (edge-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut must be at least 3. Since I've already found a cut of size 3, this would prove 3 is the minimum.

4.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths between H and N:**
    Let's try to find three paths that do not share any common edges.
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: {(H, B), (B, N)}
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}
    *   **Path 3:** H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N
        Edges: {(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)}

5.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    Let's list all edges used in these three paths:
    *   Path 1: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   Path 2: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   Path 3: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these three paths are unique and do not overlap. Therefore, these are three distinct edge-disjoint paths between H and N.

6.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.
    As we identified a cut set of 3 edges (e.g., [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)]) that successfully disconnects N from the rest of the graph (and thus from H), this confirms that 3 is indeed the minimum number of edges.

7.  **Select a minimal cut set:**
    The cut set [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)] effectively isolates node N, ensuring no path exists to H.

2.  **Analyze Connections from M:** Node M is directly connected to L, E, and J.
3.  **Analyze Paths from M:**
    *   Any path starting from M must begin with M-L, M-E, or M-J.
    *   If a path starts with M-L, the next node must be E (as L is only connected to E among the white nodes that lead towards B). So, paths starting M-L effectively pass through E.
    *   Therefore, every path from M to B must pass through either node E or node J. There is no other way to leave the immediate vicinity of M and proceed towards B without touching E or J.
4.  **Formulate a Cut Set:**
    *   If we remove node E, all paths that go through E (including those starting M-L-E) are broken. Any remaining path from M to B must then go through J.
    *   If we remove node J, all paths that go through J are broken. Any remaining path from M to B must then go through E (or L then E).
    *   Therefore, removing both E and J will ensure that no path exists between M and B. This forms a cut set: [E, J].
5.  **Check for Smaller Cut Sets (Minimum Number of Nodes):**
    *   **Size 1:** We need to verify if any single node removal can disconnect M and B.
        *   Removing L: Paths like M-E-H-B and M-J-I-B still exist.
        *   Removing E: Paths like M-J-I-B and M-J-P-C-B still exist.
        *   Removing J: Paths like M-E-H-B and M-L-E-I-B still exist.
        *   Removing H: Paths like M-E-I-B and M-J-I-B still exist.
        *   Removing I: Paths like M-E-H-B and M-J-P-C-B still exist.
        *   Removing C: Paths like M-E-H-B and M-J-I-B still exist.
        *   Removing N: Paths like M-E-H-B and M-J-I-B still exist.
        *   (And so on for other nodes like P, D, F, G, K, X, O, Y - none of them are single cut nodes).
    *   Since no single node removal disconnects M and B, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.
6.  **Conclusion:** We found a cut set of size 2 ([E, J]), and we've established that a cut set of size 1 is not possible. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]

    *   Neighbors of N: Y, O, B, C
3.  **Find Vertex-Disjoint Paths:** To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove (the minimum vertex cut), I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between H and N. The number of such paths provides a lower bound for the cut set size (Menger's Theorem).
    *   **Path 1:** H - C - N. (Intermediate node: C)
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - J - M - O - N. (Intermediate nodes: I, J, M, O). This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1, as C is not in {I, J, M, O}.
    *   **Path 3:** H - X - K - F - E - B - N. (Intermediate nodes: X, K, F, E, B). This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (C) and Path 2 ({I, J, M, O}), as none of these nodes overlap.

    Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

4.  **Propose a Cut Set of Size 3:** Based on the paths identified, the nodes C, O, and B appear to be critical "bottlenecks" for these three disjoint paths. Let's test the cut set [C, O, B].

5.  **Verify Disconnection with Cut Set [C, O, B]:**
    *   **Paths through C:** The path H - C - N is directly broken by removing C.

    *   **Paths through Y:** Node Y is connected to N and O. Since O is removed, Y becomes isolated from the rest of the graph (except N). Therefore, no path from H can reach Y (and thus N) if O is removed.

    By removing C, O, and B, all identified paths from H to N are severed. Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, and we found a set of 3 nodes that achieves disconnection, this is the minimum.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H (black) and E (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from E.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node H is connected to: I, C, X, G.
    *   Node E is connected to: B, F, L.

2.  **Analyze all possible paths between H and E:**
    Any path from H to E must eventually pass through one of the nodes directly connected to E (i.e., B, F, or L) and then use the corresponding edge to reach E.
    Let's identify distinct "channels" or "entry points" to E from H:

    *   **Channel 1: Paths through node B to E (using edge (B, E))**
        Examples:
        *   H - C - B - E
        *   H - I - C - B - E
        *   H - C - O - N - B - E
        *   H - I - J - P - O - N - B - E (and many others via O, N, Y, C, I, J, P)

    *   **Channel 2: Paths through node F to E (using edge (F, E))**
        Examples:
        *   H - G - F - E
        *   H - X - K - F - E
        *   H - X - G - F - E

    *   **Channel 3: Paths through node L to E (using edge (L, E))**
        Example:
        *   H - I - J - M - L - E

3.  **Identify potential cut edges:**
    To disconnect H from E, we must ensure that no path exists between them. This means we need to cut all the identified channels.
    The most direct way to cut these channels at their point of entry to E is to remove the edges (B, E), (F, E), and (L, E).

4.  **Evaluate the proposed cut set:**
    *   If we remove the edge (B, E), all paths from H that go through B to reach E are broken.
    *   If we remove the edge (F, E), all paths from H that go through F to reach E are broken.
    *   If we remove the edge (L, E), all paths from H that go through L to reach E are broken.

    By removing these three edges, node E becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore, no path can exist from H to E. This is a valid cut set of size 3: [(B, E), (F, E), (L, E)].

5.  **Determine if a smaller cut set exists (e.g., size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Size 1:** It's clear that removing any single edge will not disconnect H and E, as there are multiple independent paths. For example, removing (B, E) would still leave paths through F and L.
    *   **Size 2:** Let's consider removing any two edges.
        *   If we remove (B, E) and (F, E), the path H - I - J - M - L - E still exists.
        *   If we remove (B, E) and (L, E), the path H - G - F - E still exists.
        *   If we remove (F, E) and (L, E), the path H - C - B - E still exists.
        Since removing any two of the edges (B, E), (F, E), (L, E) leaves at least one path intact, a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient, and we have found a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects H and E, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, E), (F, E), (L, E)]

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and H. I cannot remove E or H.

First, I will identify all distinct paths from E to H. To do this systematically, I'll consider the immediate neighbors of E and H.
Nodes directly connected to E: M, L, F, B.
Nodes directly connected to H: B, G, I.

Notice that B is a common neighbor to both E and H.

Let's list the main "branches" or "entry points" from E towards H, and the "exit points" from the graph towards H.

**Paths from E to H can be categorized by their initial step from E:**

1.  **Paths starting with E -> B:**
    *   E - B - H (Direct path)
    *   E - B - C - I - H
    *   E - B - C - O - P - I - H
    *   E - B - N - C - I - H
    *   E - B - N - C - O - P - I - H
    *   E - B - N - O - P - I - H
    *   E - B - N - Y - O - P - I - H
    All these paths pass through node B.

2.  **Paths starting with E -> F:**
    *   E - F - K - G - H
    *   E - F - X - K - G - H
    All these paths pass through node F (and then K and G).

3.  **Paths starting with E -> M:**
    *   E - M - J - D - P - I - H
    (Note: E-M-L-E is a cycle and L does not lead to H without going back to E or through M).
    This path passes through node M.

To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I look for node-disjoint paths between E and H. If I find 'k' node-disjoint paths (excluding E and H), then I know that at least 'k' nodes must be removed.

Let's identify three node-disjoint paths:
*   **Path 1:** E - B - H
    *   Intermediate nodes: {B}
*   **Path 2:** E - F - K - G - H
    *   Intermediate nodes: {F, K, G}
*   **Path 3:** E - M - J - D - P - I - H
    *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, D, P, I}

Let's check if the sets of intermediate nodes for these three paths are disjoint:
*   {B} is disjoint from {F, K, G}.
*   {B} is disjoint from {M, J, D, P, I}.
*   {F, K, G} is disjoint from {M, J, D, P, I}.
Yes, these three paths are node-disjoint (except for the source E and target H).

According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect E from H is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between E and H. Since I have found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3 that actually disconnects E and H.
Consider removing one node from each of these three node-disjoint paths.
A possible cut set would be [B, G, I]. Let's verify if removing these three nodes disconnects E from H:

1.  **Removing B:** This breaks all paths that go through B, including E-B-H and all paths like E-B-C-I-H, E-B-N-C-I-H, etc.
2.  **Removing G:** This breaks all paths that go through G, specifically E-F-K-G-H and E-F-X-K-G-H.
3.  **Removing I:** This breaks all paths that go through I, specifically E-M-J-D-P-I-H and any remaining paths from the B-branch that might have reached I (though B is already removed).

By removing B, G, and I, all identified paths from E to H are broken. Since these three nodes represent critical points in the three main "exit routes" to H (B, G, I), no path can exist between E and H after their removal.

Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, and we found a set of 3 nodes that achieves the disconnection, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, G, I]

    Removing (D, J) and (D, P) involves removing 2 edges.

5.  **Determine if a smaller cut is possible:**
    To disconnect D from the rest of the graph, at least one edge from each of its connections must be removed. Since D has two distinct connections (to J and to P), removing only one edge (e.g., (D, J)) would still leave a path through the other connection (e.g., via (D, P)). Therefore, it is impossible to disconnect D with only 1 edge removal. This implies that the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

6.  **Verify that the chosen cut set breaks all paths:**
    *   Any path from F to D that goes through J (e.g., F-E-M-J-D, F-E-B-H-J-D, F-X-K-G-H-J-D, F-K-G-H-J-D) will be broken by the removal of edge (J, D).
    *   Any path from F to D that goes through P (e.g., F-E-B-N-Y-O-P-D, F-E-B-N-C-I-P-D, F-E-B-H-I-P-D, F-X-K-G-H-I-P-D, F-K-G-H-I-P-D) will be broken by the removal of edge (P, D).
    Since all paths from F to D must use either (J, D) or (P, D), removing both edges ensures that no path exists between F and D.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges to remove is 2, and the cut set consists of the two edges directly connected to node D.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(D, J), (D, P)]

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and B.

First, I identify all possible paths between H and B:
1.  **Direct Path:** H - B
2.  **Path via G and E:** H - G - F - E - B
3.  **Path via G, X, F, and E:** H - G - X - F - E - B
4.  **Path via I and C:** H - I - C - B
5.  **Path via I, P, O, and C:** H - I - P - O - C - B
6.  **Path via I, P, O, and N:** H - I - P - O - N - B

To disconnect H and B, every single one of these paths must be broken.

**Strategy: Consider the edges directly connected to H (excluding B).**
The edges directly connected to H are (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I).
If I remove all these three edges, H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B.
Let's test this cut set: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)].
*   Removing (H, B) breaks the direct path (1).
*   Removing (H, G) breaks all paths that start with H-G (paths 2 and 3).
*   Removing (H, I) breaks all paths that start with H-I (paths 4, 5, and 6).
This set of 3 edges successfully disconnects H from B.

**Can we do it with fewer than 3 edges?**
Let's try to prove that 2 edges are not sufficient.
Assume we can disconnect H and B by removing only two edges, say `e1` and `e2`.

**Case 1: The direct edge (H, B) is NOT removed.**
If (H, B) is not removed, then H and B are still connected, which contradicts the goal. Therefore, if we are to disconnect them, (H, B) must either be removed, or all alternative paths must be cut. If (H, B) is not removed, then we must cut all paths from H-G to B and all paths from H-I to B using only `e1` and `e2`.
*   Paths from H-G to B (2 and 3) both pass through E.
*   Paths from H-I to B (4, 5, and 6) pass through C or N.
These two sets of paths (from G to B and from I to B) are largely independent after leaving G and I. No single edge can cut all paths from G to B AND all paths from I to B. For example, removing (E, B) would cut paths from G, but not from I. Removing (C, B) would cut some paths from I, but not from G, and not H-I-P-O-N-B. Thus, it's impossible to cut all these paths with just two edges if (H, B) is not removed.

**Case 2: The direct edge (H, B) IS removed.**
So, `e1` = (H, B). Now we have only one more edge, `e2`, to remove.
After removing (H, B), we still have paths from H to B via G and I:
*   Paths H-G-F-E-B and H-G-X-F-E-B.
*   Paths H-I-C-B, H-I-P-O-C-B, and H-I-P-O-N-B.
A single edge `e2` cannot cut all these remaining paths. If `e2` cuts a path from the H-G branch (e.g., (E, B)), it will not affect the H-I branch. If `e2` cuts a path from the H-I branch (e.g., (C, B)), it will not affect the H-G branch, and might not even cut all H-I paths (e.g., H-I-P-O-N-B would remain).
Therefore, two edges are not enough to disconnect H and B.

Since 2 edges are not enough, and we found a valid cut set of 3 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

The cut set [(H, B), (G, H), (H, I)] is one such minimal set.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (G, H), (H, I)]

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 7
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of J, as any path from E to J must pass through one of these nodes. The neighbors of J are M, K, I, P, and D.

Let's list the "channels" or "groups of paths" from E to J, categorized by the last node before J:

1.  **Paths through M to J:**
    *   E - M - J
    *   E - L - M - J
    All these paths can be cut by removing the edge (M, J).

2.  **Paths through K to J:**
    *   E - F - K - J
    *   E - F - X - K - J
    *   E - G - K - J
    All these paths can be cut by removing the edge (K, J).

3.  **Paths through I to J:**
    *   E - G - H - I - J
    *   E - B - C - I - J
    *   E - B - N - C - I - J
    All these paths can be cut by removing the edge (I, J).

4.  **Paths through P to J:**
    *   E - B - C - O - P - J
    *   E - B - N - C - O - P - J
    *   E - B - N - Y - O - P - J
    These paths can be cut by removing the edge (P, J).

5.  **Paths through D to J:**
    *   E - B - C - O - P - D - J
    *   E - B - N - C - O - P - D - J
    *   E - B - N - Y - O - P - D - J
    These paths can be cut by removing the edge (D, J).

If we remove all edges directly connected to J, i.e., [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J)], we would disconnect J from the rest of the graph. This is a cut of size 5.

Now, I look for a smaller cut by identifying if any single edge can cut multiple groups of paths.
Let's re-examine groups 4 and 5 (paths through P and D).
*   Node D is connected only to J and P.
*   All paths from E to P must pass through node O (e.g., E-B-C-O-P, E-B-N-C-O-P, E-B-N-Y-O-P).
*   Therefore, if the edge (O, P) is removed, no path from E can reach P.
*   Since D is only connected to J and P, if P is unreachable from E, then D is also unreachable from E (except through J, which is the target node).
*   This means that removing the single edge (O, P) effectively disconnects both P and D from the E-side of the graph. Consequently, all paths from E to J that would go through P or D are broken by removing (O, P).

So, instead of removing (P, J) and (D, J) (2 edges), we can remove (O, P) (1 edge) to achieve the same effect for paths through P and D.

This leads to the following candidate cut set:
1.  **(M, J)**: Cuts all paths through M.
2.  **(K, J)**: Cuts all paths through K.
3.  **(I, J)**: Cuts all paths through I.
4.  **(O, P)**: Cuts all paths through P and D.

This cut set consists of 4 edges: [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (O, P)].

**Verification:**
*   After removing (M, J), no path E -> ... -> M -> J exists.
*   After removing (K, J), no path E -> ... -> K -> J exists.
*   After removing (I, J), no path E -> ... -> I -> J exists.
*   After removing (O, P), no path E -> ... -> P -> J exists (because P is disconnected from the E-side). Also, no path E -> ... -> D -> J exists (because D is only connected to J and P, and P is disconnected from the E-side).

All identified path groups are disconnected. This cut set of 4 edges is effective.

**Minimality Check:**
Each of the four chosen edges (M, J), (K, J), (I, J), and (O, P) is essential because it cuts a distinct "channel" of paths that is not cut by the other three.
*   (M, J) is the only edge in this set that cuts paths through M.
*   (K, J) is the only edge in this set that cuts paths through K.
*   (I, J) is the only edge in this set that cuts paths through I.
*   (O, P) is the only edge in this set that cuts paths through P and D.
If any one of these 4 edges were not removed, a path from E to J would still exist through its respective channel. Therefore, a cut of size 3 is not possible.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (O, P)]
