    Removing all edges connected to D (P,D), (J,D), (O,D) would isolate D, requiring 3 edges. We need to check if a smaller cut is possible.

2.  **Find edge-disjoint paths:**
    According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (edge-cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from F to D.

    *   **Path 1:** F - G - H - I - P - D
        Edges: {(F,G), (G,H), (H,I), (I,P), (P,D)}

    *   **Path 2:** F - E - B - C - O - D
        Edges: {(F,E), (E,B), (B,C), (C,O), (O,D)}
        These two paths are edge-disjoint as they do not share any common edges.

    *   **Attempt to find a third edge-disjoint path:**
        *   From F, the remaining unused edges are (F,K) and (F,X).
        *   Consider a path starting F - K - X. From X, the only connection is to G. So, F - K - X - G.
            However, the edge (G,H) is used by Path 1. If we use (G,H), this path is not edge-disjoint from Path 1.
            Node G is connected to F, X, H. (G,F) is a back-edge to F. (G,X) leads to X, which is connected to K and G. So, any path from F through K or X must eventually pass through G. If (G,H) is used by Path 1, then any path from F through K or X that wants to reach the "middle" part of the graph (H, I, B, C, N) must use (G,H).
        *   Consider a path through M. Node M is connected to E, J, L. To reach M from F, we must use the edge (F,E).
            However, the edge (F,E) is used by Path 2. Therefore, any path through M (e.g., F - E - M - J - D) would not be edge-disjoint from Path 2.

    Since we could only find two edge-disjoint paths, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 2. By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

3.  **Identify a minimal cut set of 2 edges:**
    We need to find two edges whose removal will break all paths between F and D. Based on the analysis of edge-disjoint paths, we need to find two edges that effectively cut off the two main "branches" originating from F.

    Let's consider the edges (G,H) and (F,E).

    *   **If we remove (G,H):**
        *   Node G is connected to F, X, H.
        *   If (G,H) is removed, G can only reach F or X.
        *   From X, it can only reach K or G.
        *   From K, it can only reach F or X.
        *   This means that if (G,H) is removed, the subgraph containing {F, K, X, G} becomes disconnected from the rest of the graph. Therefore, all paths starting from F that go through G, K, or X are broken. This includes Path 1 (F - G - H - I - P - D) and any other path that uses the F-K-X-G branch.

    *   **If we remove (F,E):**
        *   All paths starting from F that use the edge (F,E) are broken. This includes Path 2 (F - E - B - C - O - D) and any other path that uses the F-E branch (e.g., F - E - M - J - D).

    **Verification:**
    *   Any path from F must start with one of its direct connections: (F,K), (F,X), (F,G), or (F,E).
    *   If a path starts with (F,E), it is broken by the removal of (F,E).
    *   If a path starts with (F,K), it must go F-K-X-G. From G, it cannot proceed to H because (G,H) is removed. Thus, it cannot reach D.
    *   If a path starts with (F,X), it must go F-X-G. From G, it cannot proceed to H because (G,H) is removed. Thus, it cannot reach D.
    *   If a path starts with (F,G), it cannot proceed to H because (G,H) is removed. Thus, it cannot reach D.

    Therefore, removing the edges (G,H) and (F,E) completely disconnects F from D. This cut set has 2 edges, which matches the minimum cut size determined by Menger's theorem.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, H), (F, E)]

- The only remaining path is L-M-J (path 1).
- If M is also removed, this path is broken.
- Therefore, removing E and M disconnects L from J. This is a cut set of size 2.

Both [M, P] and [E, M] are valid cut sets of size 2. Since we have established that a single node removal is not enough, 2 is the minimum.

    *   (I, H) - for all paths entering H via I.

    *   Path 3 uses (B, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H). None of these are in Path 1 or Path 2.
